Do this crucial thing to thrive during unemployment

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A job search, whatever the length, is demoralizing.

After losing a job, you’ll start asking yourself challenging questions about finances, childcare and more. You may also feel the pang of an identity crisis—particularly if you’re a longtime employee whose daily life has become tied up with work.

On top of that, there’s the hours of applying, networking, interviewing, and dealing with modern-day job hunt maladies, from AI and ghost jobs. Lack of communication and recruiter bait-and-switch make the process feel useless. 

The mental battle can be a monster. But experts suggest a possible sanity-saving sword to slay that monster: the power of having a routine. That can be key, especially when navigating a process that may drag on past six months or more.

Melissa Dorman, an organizational psychologist and executive coach, shares that in today’s anonymous market, “no matter how much you get done, you can feel behind or get sucked into the vortex of applying for one job after another.”

Crafting (and sticking) to a routine during this period is not only a way to stay positive during a job search—but it could also be a means to open up job seekers to new opportunities.

Routines and productivity

The salve to some of this strangeness may come down to how seekers control what is within their power. A routine can serve as that support and stability that can put a period of uncontrollable unpredictability into perspective. 

“Stability is everything when your world feels like the bottom has fallen out, especially when losing a job and searching for a job in a volatile market,” says Dorman.

By setting routines, what matters is, “you can know how your day will start tomorrow morning.”

As Dorman recommends, try to set specific times for job applications. Outside of that time, focus on developing new skills, conducting networking outreach, or simply giving yourself some grace to reconnect with what brings you joy. These new activities can not only bring you joy—but serve as anchors throughout your day. Over time, you may see that your routine gives you purpose.

“One sanity-protecting exercise is to implement an ‘office hours’ approach for apps,” adds Dorman, who frequently coaches managers on keeping their skills sharp. 

With these kinds of consistent routines, you can free up mental space so you are able to meet the perfect opportunity when it presents itself: “You can’t be productive or maintain motivation long-term if [only] completing job applications is your full-time job.”

Benefits of consistency

Routines can be tricky to make stick, though—the human brain is wired to feel lit up by novel experiences.

“Our brains love new things. When we start a routine, we get a bit of a dopamine hit, or a burst of the motivational chemical that craves rewards. But when the routine becomes familiar, what once felt exciting starts to feel like work,” says Dorman.

That’s why being intentionally consistent is key: blocking off time on your calendar for that 4:30 p.m. jog, or booking a hot desk at your favorite coworking space, where you specifically research interesting companies or dig into building new skills.

With repetition, not only does the craving for novelty become less pronounced, but the routine itself starts to reinforce itself from positive associations. Your weekly workout may suddenly be the most significant time to relax and refresh yourself, for example.

Make time in the first few weeks to feel a little uncomfortable when cementing your new routine—but stick to a regular schedule. And if you’re comfortable sharing your new routine, recruit a close friend to keep you accountable through regular check-ins.

“The more often you do something, the less you have to think about it,” says Dorman.

Being consistent also makes us feel more productive. According to long-cited research by Teresa Amabile, a psychologist and professor emerita at Harvard Business School, the act of making progress on a project of any size involving personal investment leads to feelings of productivity. Her team dubbed this concept the “progress principle.” Amabile’s research shows this ability to make even a small dent on a project improves workers’ motivation, emotional health, and sense of personal performance. The frequency of these feelings also affected the duration subjects remained productive.

In essence, building up wins throughout the day equaled a feeling of motivation for people over the long-term.

Crafting your successful routine

  • 1. Make it a habit


Habits are borne from planning ahead. One place to start when you’re still looking for work? Take an audit of your sleep schedule. Laura Vanderkam, a Fast Company contributor who has written about productive scheduling, stresses the importance of a consistent sleep schedule in her 2022 book, Tranquility by Tuesday.

Unorganized sleep schedules are hidden saboteurs, causing people to crash out and squander productivity in order to make up for rest. It’s too easy to get into a cycle of stretching yourself thin during the work week—only to oversleep on weekends and throw off your rhythm on weekdays. With a consistent sleep and wake-up time, you not only regulate your flow of energy for the day, but you’re setting yourself up for success tomorrow on your terms.

  • 2. Place rewards alongside hard work

Harvard behavioral scientists Katy Milkman, Julia Minson, and Kevin Volpp point to the practice of bundling temptations alongside tedious or daunting work to fuel motivation. Pair activities that are a chore or bore you with a motivational pick-me-up—like your favorite coffee or a few minutes of vinyasa flow.

And Thomas Davies, a previous director at Google for Work and Fast Company contributor, writes that engaging in activities that genuinely light you up will make an onerous or soulless job search feel more fruitful or at least seem like one step to something better.

MIT researchers also draw a connection between the importance of mental hints when developing habits. They claim habits are often associated with a physical cue that triggers a habit response—so go ahead and reach for your noise-cancelling headphones or shift over to your standing desk when hard work calls.

  • 3. Don’t ignore the paths of least resistance

Finally, keeping a routine doesn’t mean being inflexible.

According to a 2026 study carried out by Columbia researchers, people often get stuck in inertia (fixed mindsets, anyone?), which can cause them to disregard new pathways to their goal. Sometimes, the more interesting route can be more fun and lead you to the same result. So stay open to changing your set routines when it feels right. Maybe this means changing the ratio of your networking efforts to application efforts. Maybe this means searching for skills over strict job titles. 

Whatever the shift looks like, the idea applies to before you get the job… and when you land it.

Keep these tips to make your routine a steady one—and to make your unemployment period a productive one.

How to answer Claude Code or Codex from your phone

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 10 • Originally published at ponymux.com

You start an agent on the computer at home and leave. An hour later your phone shows that it stopped to ask for permission to run a command. It stays stopped until you answer, and you're nowhere near the desk.

Each of the big agents has a way to answer from your phone. They just don't work the same way:

Use Remote Control, Claude Code's own feature. In a session you've already started, type:

(/rc is the short form.) The session keeps running on your computer, with your files, MCP servers, and settings. Your phone only shows it and sends messages. Open the session by name in the Claude app under Code, or at claude.ai/code. A session that's online shows a computer icon with a green dot.

To be told when it needs you, open /config and turn on Push when actions required. Install the Claude app and sign in with the same account.

The full list is in Anthropic's Remote Control documentation.

Codex does it through the ChatGPT apps, and setup starts on the computer, not in the terminal. You can't set it up from the Codex CLI or the IDE extension.

From the phone you can continue chats, answer questions, approve commands, and read diffs and terminal output. The work still runs on the computer, with its files and tools.

The computer must be awake, online, and signed in. On a Mac laptop, remote access can stay available with the lid open and power connected; with the lid closed it also needs an external display, and choosing Sleep ends it. Each phone is paired with each computer it controls. The rest is in OpenAI's remote connections documentation.

Yes, and it's the one method that doesn't care which agent you run. You connect to the computer, then attach to a terminal where the agent is already running. It takes more setup than the two above.

On the Mac, turn on SSH: open System Settings → General → Sharing, then turn on Remote Login. Under Allow access for, choose Only these users and add yourself.

From outside your home network you also need a way to reach the Mac. Don't open its SSH port to the internet. Use a VPN or a mesh network such as Tailscale, so only your own devices can connect.

Then run the agent inside tmux, which keeps the terminal alive when you disconnect:

Anthropic's own documentation suggests the same for Claude Code over SSH: use tmux or screen so the session survives a dropped connection.

This works, but it's the roughest on a phone. You're typing into a full terminal with a touch keyboard, and the keys an agent needs, Esc, Tab, Ctrl, and the arrows, aren't on it. You also set up the SSH app, the network, and the Mac yourself.

Almost always, the computer went to sleep. Locking the screen doesn't stop an agent; idle sleep does, and so does closing the lid. Remote Control for Claude Code and Codex, and SSH, can only reach a computer that's awake and online.

None of the agents keep the Mac awake between turns, and that's exactly when you're waiting to answer from the phone. How to keep your Mac awake for Claude Code and Codex covers it step by step.

Then there's no single place to answer. Claude Code's remote lives in the Claude app, Codex's in the ChatGPT app, and a Grok session on your own computer has no remote from xAI that I could find in its documentation. Over SSH you can reach all three, but you have to find the right tmux session yourself and read the terminal to see which one is waiting.

With one agent, any of the methods above is fine. With three, the problem is no longer how to connect, it's which one is waiting for you.

Yes. PonyMux runs the real CLI of each agent in its own Terminal, so Remote Control doesn't depend on any agent offering its own remote. It's free on macOS and Windows.

One device controls an agent at a time. On the computer, a Stop button takes control back whenever you want.

What it doesn't do: it doesn't wake a sleeping computer, so Keep Awake is worth turning on before you leave. There's no end-to-end encryption, and no approval cards that answer for you; you read the Terminal and type the answer. The server relays the screen and keystrokes and saves none of them. On the Free plan, one computer shares its agents at a time.

For setup, see Set up Remote Control. Remote Control on your phone covers the key bar and notifications, and How Remote Control stays safe lists exactly what is and isn't protected.

Store Timestamps in UTC, Display Them Locally

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A CRM stores '10:30' beside a follow-up task. One representative reads it in Jaipur and another in London. Neither knows which timezone the value represents.

Time data needs to describe either an instant or a local scheduling intention. Those are different requirements.

An enquiry acceptance time is an instant. Store it using a timezone-aware database type or an explicit UTC representation supported by your system.

JavaScript can serialize a valid Date as a UTC ISO string:

This records the browser or server clock's value. For authoritative acceptance time, prefer the backend's timestamp and maintain its clock synchronization.

Do not infer an instant from an ambiguous string such as 06/07/2026 10:30.

Display an instant in an explicit timezone using a locale-aware formatter:

Choose the display timezone from a documented user or application setting. Label it where confusion is possible.

'Call tomorrow at 9 AM in the customer's timezone' contains more than an instant. Store the selected local date and time together with an IANA timezone identifier, then resolve it for execution.

Recurring local schedules need their timezone rules preserved. A fixed UTC offset does not represent future daylight-saving changes.

Some local times occur twice when clocks move backward; others do not occur when clocks move forward. Ask for or document a resolution policy rather than silently choosing an arbitrary instant.

A delayed event's receipt time is not its occurrence time. Avoid overwriting one with another.

Test different viewer timezones, month and year boundaries, daylight-saving transitions for relevant regions, invalid dates, and absent timezone settings.

The conversion code above is illustrative and should be verified in your target runtime. Consistent timestamps make routing, follow-ups, and incident investigation easier because everyone can identify which moment a record describes.

NFL Week 10 power rankings: Despite 4 straight wins, Detroit Lions sit behind NFC North rivals

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Detroit Lions (6-2) have now won four in a row, are 2-0 in the division with wins over the Packers and Vikings, and sit in second place in the NFC North, just a half-game back of the Chicago Bears. For the first time this season, the Lions rank in the top 10 of every national power ranking that we track.

But it's not all sunshine and roses in the Lions' relationship with the national media. Of the eight rankings we track, the Lions dropped this week in over half of them. Even more surprising, they dropped behind the Packers in two (Yahoo Sports and Sports Illustrated) and remain behind the Vikings in two others (Sporting News and ESPN)—despite having the same (Vikings) or better (Packers) record—and winning both head-to-head matchups.

Let's take a closer look at this week's national power rankings.

Having spent time in Buffalo after Damar Hamlin's near-death experience in a Bills-Bengals game, I can still only begin to fathom the heaviness this Lions team is dealing with on a daily basis in the aftermath of the Jack Campbell injury. It makes the Dolphins victory feel very different… and somehow more amazing.

WR Amon-Ra St. Brown hasn't scored since September and only has two 100-yard receiving games this season. Think the German-American star might break out Sunday in Munich − especially given how the Pats secondary just got shredded?

Sunday's victory wasn't pretty by any means, but sometimes a team needs to find a way to win an ugly one. The Lions did that in Miami, getting just enough from Jahmyr Gibbs to leave with a W. That's what I'd expect from a team in the top 10, but I'm hoping this was an informative experience to grow from, not a harbinger of things to come.

It hasn't always looked pretty (including in Miami on Sunday), and the underlying defensive stats (they rank 30th in EPA per play) remain concerning. But the offense has been among the game's best as they now head abroad to face the reeling New England defense.

It wasn't pretty against the Dolphins, but they found a way. They can't play that way this week against a wounded Patriots team or else.

Is the Lions' defense good? It has been better recently. For the third straight game the Lions' opponent failed to top 17 points. Two of those games happened against the offensively challenged Vikings and Dolphins; the third game in that streak was against the Packers. Advanced metrics are kind to the Lions' defense over the past three weeks too, as Detroit is eighth in EPA per play allowed and fifth in success rate allowed. Star safety Brian Branch returned from an Achilles injury three games ago, and it's not a coincidence the Lions' defense is much better with him back. The Lions have held opponents to 21 or fewer points in four straight games, the first time that has happened since 2016, according to the team's site.

The Lions allowed just 228 yards and 12 first downs Sunday, and while that was against Miami, it's still a lot better than Detroit looked on defense early in the season.

"We've cleaned a lot up," Lions head coach Dan Campbell said, via the team's site. "Guys are trusting what they're being taught, they're executing it and with that execution comes confidence."

From ESPN Staff, excerpt from Eric Woodyard:

International games are rare for the Lions, who haven't played outside the U.S. since 2015. And this matchup in Munich is a homecoming of sorts for WR Amon-Ra St. Brown, whose mother is German and who has spent considerable time in the country. This contest also marks Detroit's first game in Germany and the first time it is the designated home team for an international game, so this is certainly a huge deal. "You kind of need that extra story to tell," NFL Germany GM Alexander Steinforth told ESPN. "You need that extra link into the market to have a really meaningful impact, and that's Amon-Ra St. Brown."

The Lions' once-maligned defense has allowed only a total of 62 points during their four-game winning streak. They will get a good test against the Patriots in Munich, Germany, in Week 10, where Jared Goff needs to shake off back-to-back sub 200-yard, one-INT games.

Il retroscena su Cisse: c'era anche chi non lo voleva

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

È il milanista del momento senza dubbio. Otto gol, "I love Cisse" e la prima convocazione in nazionale maggiore. Che Alphadjo Cisse in così poco tempo si affermasse in questo modo nel Milan in pochi potevano immaginarlo. Anzi, c'è chi a gennaio non era poi così contento del suo acquisto, per diversi motivi. Alcuni probabilmente anche condivisibili. Ma andiamo con ordine.

Perché è giusto parlare delle giocate e dei gol che stanno facendo gioire i tifosi rossoneri, ma è importante sapere tutto quello che c'è dietro. A gennaio 2026 Cisse sembrava destinato - biglietto di sola andata - al PSV. Una telefonata del Milan ha cambiato tutto: il classe 2006, che stava giocando in prestito al Catanzaro dal Verona, blocca tutto e vuole solo rossonero. Il Diavolo convince il Verona con 8 milioni di euro e convince il ragazzo con un progetto di crescita - probabilmente tra i pochissimi riusciti nell'ultimo periodo - giusto. Si decide che può rimanere in prestito in Serie B, dove stava iniziando ad ingranare, per poi aggregarsi al resto della squadra a Milanello a fine stagione. Si fa tutto in pochissimo tempo: Moncada, ai tempi DT, stravede per lui e Giorgio Furlani chiude velocemente l'operazione col "solito" Busardò, agente del giocatore con la sua CAA Base.

Il retroscena che può raccontare la redazione di MilanNews.it è che in quei giorni di fine gennaio, quando il Milan sembrava poter avere ancora qualche velleità di Scudetto, Massimiliano Allegri prese molto male l'allocamento di parte del budget per andare a bloccare il giovane Cisse, che per di più non sarebbe arrivato in rossonero prima di sei mesi. A gennaio l'allenatore toscano aveva chiesto rinforzi di tutt'altra risma, mentre arrivò solo Fullkrug in prestito dal West Ham. Il mercato sembrava bloccato, bloccatissimo, e un investimento importante per un classe 2006 che non aveva mai messo piede in Serie A aveva totalmente contrariato Allegri: il livornese lo considerava un rinforzo al massimo per il Milan Futuro e non mancò di far filtrare fastidio e negatività per l'operazione poi conclusa dal CEO Furlani.

Da una parte può essere anche comprensibile l'atteggiamento dell'ex allenatore del Milan: a gennaio i rossoneri erano nelle zone alte della classifica e si sognava un clamoroso colpaccio scudetto. Allegri si sarebbe aspettato rinforzi per l'immediato e per competere fin da subito per dare filo da torcere all'Inter, cosa che poi non è successa.

Però, da qui a voler osteggiare l'ingaggio di un giovane potenzialmente molto forte sarebbe stato importante anche trovare una via di mezzo. Cisse alla fine è arrivato e i sei mesi ulteriori in prestito al Catanzaro, nonostante il grave infortunio, probabilmente hanno fatto tutta la differenza del mondo. Gettare l'ennesimo ragazzo nelle fiamme di un Milan in confusione sarebbe stato il solito bagno di sangue. A maggio c'è stato un reset totale da parte di Cardinale che ha azzerato tutto. Il numero uno di RedBird ha poi insistito su come ci dovrà essere sempre più accortezza nel maneggiare i giovani rossoneri: se con Camarda e Comotto siamo ancora work in progress, il percorso di Cisse, ma anche quello di Bartesaghi e Diawara, è un netto taglio rispetto al recente, caotico, passato.

Ora il classe 2006 ha una maglia che ha dimostrato di poter indossare, un allenatore che crede molto in lui - "I love Cisse" estivo di Amorim è già storia - e un propietario attento alla crescita dei giovani. Evidentemente Allegri, pur con le sue ragioni, aveva sbagliato completamente la valutazione. Cisse ha tutto per poter diventare un calciatore veramente importante e non solo un giovane dal bel potenziale. Ma vedendo come parla, come si allena - in estate, quando lavorava a Milanello per recuperare dall'infortunio, rimaneva a guardare il lavoro di Amorim con la squadra per capire subito cosa volesse - e come gioca c'è da essere ottimisti.

Reynolds: Fearless Love can lead Packers to Super Bowl

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Sky Sports NFL's Neil Reynolds hails the form of Jordan Love and the Green Bay Packers in his weekly column; the Packers improved to 6-3 on the year on Sunday with a dominant win over the New England Patriots to continue their march towards the NFL playoffs

I'm well aware that we've only reached the halfway point of the 2026 season and so much can change in the NFL, especially at the back end of the year.

But I'm now starting to believe the Green Bay Packers can make a serious run and they have emerged as true contenders in the NFC.

Sunday's 45-27 win over the New England Patriots in Foxborough was a statement victory and it was a win that showed the Packers to be a complete team.

Jordan Love is playing as well as any quarterback in the NFL this season and he backed it up on Sunday on a big stage in a game being called by Tom Brady as he threw for 372 yards, three touchdowns and no interceptions.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

He could not look more comfortable right now. He's in his bag, he's playing with confidence, he's playing calmly and it looks like he has no fear.

That's what's incredible when you look at the success of Jordan Love. It's not dinking and dunking, it is attacking downfield at a more prolific rate than any other quarterback in the NFL.

And he has the guys that can make the plays on the other end. Matthew Golden and Christian Watson both went over 150 receiving yards on Sunday and they both have emerged as true game-breaking wide receivers.

Now you could say that Golden went into Week Nine leading the NFL with seven drops but then look at some of the catches he's tried to make. Again I think it goes back to that aggressive nature of the Packers.

He's not catching 10-yard curl routes or 10-yard crossers over the middle. He's quite often having to track a football that's traveling 50 or 60 yards in the air with the defensive back draped all over him.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

So it wasn't that long ago we looked at the Packers with four or five wide receiver targets and said they've got a collection of players but they don't have a true number one. Well they might have something approaching two number ones now in Watson and Golden.

Defensively I felt like they've been dangerous all year and they showed it on Sunday, pouncing early for the interception of Drake Maye - who regressed on a huge stage. Of all the days for Drake Maye to have a nightmare, Tom Brady's calling the game up in the booth - a reminder of the gigantic shoes to fill in New England.

But this is what the Packers do on defense. They rush the passer really well. They harass teams into mistakes and then they are opportunistic. Four takeaways on Sunday was no fluke.

What they did in Foxborough was what they've done all season long, and they still have one of the most prolific pass rushers in the game to return providing Micah Parsons comes back at full speed.

He has taken his time coming back from the knee injury and should be targeting a Thanksgiving week (3:03) return to action.

If that boost comes at the right time then I think the Packers have all the tools to make a serious run come January and I think they're more than capable, based on what we've seen in the last couple of weeks, of extending that run all the way into February and you know what that means - the Super Bowl.

Nintendo Switch 2 emulation on Android already has The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake up and running

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake now runs surprisingly well on phones after just a week of additional emulator development.

Legend of Zelda fans all over the world have been marveling at the Ocarina of Time remake’s visuals and enhanced gameplay systems on the Nintendo Switch 2, but the Android emulator scene has been busy marveling over something just a little bit different. One Redditor got the latest Zelda game running on Banana Native, a free emulator that’s available right now on GitHub.

Ocarina of Time remake performance has now reached a major milestone into the 30 FPS territory with frame gen, which means the game is actually running at around 15 FPS, but then scaled and interpolated using AI frame generation to appear like 30 FPS. Banana Native isn’t a traditional emulator, as it doesn’t emulate hardware. Rather, it uses the game’s own ARM64 code to run on the phone’s CPU. The Switch 2 is powered by a mobile Nvidia chip, after all.

The user is running the Ocarina of Time remake on a Poco F7 Ultra, which sports a two-generation-old Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite (Gen 4) processor. The latest phones that are coming out, like the Redmagic 12 Pro, run a 6th-gen Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme chip, so performance could be a lot better on a newer phone. Apps like Banana Native require specific driver support for each phone to run, and as I pointed out in my Redmagic 12 Pro review, platforms like Droid Deck and Game Native don’t yet have proper drivers for the newest chips.

9to5Google’s Damien Wilde recently reviewed Droid Deck, another native-type emulator that lets you legally play Steam games you own on your phone by just signing into your Steam account. It works extremely well on lots of Android phones and helps showcase just how far Android emulation has come this year.

Anthropic just launched its most powerful Claude ahead of its IPO — here's why it's telling users to stick with Opus

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Not everyone needs the company's most powerful models

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic has a new AI model, and it's more powerful than the Claude most people are using for chat. And that's precisely why the company doesn't actually recommend switching to it for everyday tasks.

Claude Fable 5.2, released Monday alongside the more restricted Mythos 5.2, is designed for some of the toughest work AI can handle, including advanced coding, scientific research and complex engineering projects.

The timing is significant. Anthropic is also preparing for a massive initial public offering, putting fresh attention on how the company plans to turn increasingly powerful AI into a profitable business.

For Claude users, though, the immediate question is simpler: what can Fable 5.2 actually do, and is it worth using instead of Opus?

Fable 5.2 is designed for projects that require more than generating an email, summarizing a document or answering a question.

Anthropic says its newest model can handle complicated technical work that would otherwise require extensive human effort. During prerelease testing, its models helped engineers complete difficult software migrations, design working electronic devices and tackle advanced physics problems.

One of the more interesting applications is using Fable as an AI advisor. Rather than asking Fable to handle every task, developers can have smaller Claude models consult it when they encounter a particularly difficult problem.

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

Fable can also act as an orchestrator, coordinating smaller models and assigning them simpler tasks while it handles the more complicated work.

For everyday users, the most practical applications could include analyzing complex research, working through difficult technical problems or managing projects that involve multiple steps and large amounts of information.

Here's where Anthropic's lineup gets confusing. Despite introducing Fable 5.2 as one of its most capable models, Anthropic recommends that most people continue using Claude Opus 5.5 for everyday coding and knowledge work.

The company describes Opus as its strongest daily driver, while Fable is intended for more ambitious technical challenges. That means someone using Claude for writing, research, brainstorming or routine coding probably doesn't need to switch.

Fable becomes more interesting when a task requires sustained reasoning, complicated problem-solving or coordination between multiple AI systems.

There are also cheaper models in the lineup. Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 remain options for simpler work where speed and efficiency matter more than maximum capability.

The takeaway is that the most powerful Claude isn't automatically the best Claude for every job.

Anthropic has reduced the price of Fable compared with its previous version, but it remains more expensive than Opus.

Through the API, Fable 5.2 costs $8 per million input tokens and $40 per million output tokens. That's 20% less than Fable 5.1, although it's still twice the per-token price of Opus 5.5.

Anthropic also says the new model generates responses up to 30% faster than its predecessor. An optional fast mode offers even higher output speeds, but at twice the standard API token price.

For developers and businesses running large numbers of AI requests, those differences can add up quickly. If you run through your usage limits quickly, you might want to choose Opus 5.5 first.

For people using Claude through a subscription, access and usage limits also matter. Fable is available through supported Claude plans, although how much you can use depends on your subscription and any applicable usage credits.

Anthropic's latest model launch arrives at a particularly important moment for the company.

According to Reuters, Anthropic circulated IPO pricing information to banks before retracting it, saying the figures were inaccurate. The withdrawn proposal outlined a potential offering worth as much as $69.3 billion, although those numbers should not be treated as finalized.

The company is entering the public-market spotlight at a time when investors are scrutinizing the enormous cost of developing and operating advanced AI.

So, instead of offering one extremly powerful version of Claude for everyone, Anthropic is building a lineup designed for different types of work and different budgets.

Haiku and Sonnet can handle simpler, high-volume tasks. Opus serves as the everyday workhorse. Fable targets more demanding projects, while Mythos offers specialized capabilities to approved organizations working in fields such as cybersecurity and biomedical research.

It's a strategy that could help Anthropic serve more customers without requiring everyone to pay for its most expensive technology.

But it also creates a challenge: users need to understand why they should pay more for one model when another may already do everything they need.

As Anthropic moves toward its IPO, demonstrating that customers will pay for those additional capabilities could become just as important as achieving another impressive benchmark score.

If you're already using Claude Opus 5.5 for writing, research, brainstorming or everyday productivity, there's little reason to rush into Fable 5.2.

The new model is worth exploring if you're working on particularly difficult technical projects or tasks where existing AI models struggle to deliver reliable results.

For everyone else, Opus remains Anthropic's recommended starting point.

Why Is Everyone So Mad at Supreme’s Elliott Smith Collaboration?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Earlier this week, Supreme announced an Elliott Smith capsule collection, including a selection of jackets and T-shirts featuring artwork from across the late musician’s career, from his self-titled 1995 album to his seminal 1997 album Either/Or to 1998’s XO and 2000’s Figure 8.

The internet seems to be in a huff over this, relying on a view of Supreme and streetwear frozen in 2008 that was, even then, embarrassingly myopic. The line of thinking goes that Supreme, the brand that was founded in New York in 1994 by designer James Jebbia and that has been a staple in skate and streetwear culture ever since, is an inauthentic purveyor of the ethos represented by Smith and his music. One might cite the brand’s roughly $500 million investment from the Carlyle Group in 2017 or its association with high fashion as disqualifying characteristics. More than anything, there seems to be a sense that taking shots at a nearly 40-year-old streetwear institution is a way to prove one’s unsullied dedication to a sense of anti-consumerism. The anger is misplaced.

In a culture awash in goofy collaborations and corporate cash grabs, Supreme might be one of the few brands still operating from an actual set of beliefs. Its Elliott Smith capsule (which was made with permission from Smith’s estate) is in a long line of collaborations that Supreme has done with musicians. Its first artist collaborator was Rammellzee, the experimental rapper and visual artist whose relationship with the brand dates to 1994, the year Supreme opened. By 2005, Raekwon was posing with a Tickle Me Elmo for the first of Supreme’s commissioned portrait T-shirts. Across its music collaborations, portrait tees, and tributes, the list includes everyone from Michael Jackson to the Velvet Underground, Nirvana, Butthole Surfers, My Bloody Valentine, Daniel Johnston, Aphex Twin, and SALEM.

There have been collections with record labels like Stax, Wackies, Rap-A-Lot, Ruff Ryders, Jah Life, and Duck Down, the last of which put Black Moon and Smif-N-Wessun’s album artwork on clothes. There are shirts and shoes with Peter Saville’s artwork for Joy Division and New Order. The Smith collaboration is so patently in the wheelhouse of what the brand has done for decades that bringing it up now as some sort of “gotcha!” feels more like a product of social media’s tilt towards rage-bait than any defense of an artist. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Elliott Smith, who died in 2003 at 34, has long been a part of skate culture. He was friends with Mike Mills, the skateboarder and filmmaker who designed graphics for Stereo and one of Supreme’s early logos. Mills worked on unused cover art for Figure 8 and later enlisted Smith to record music for his film Thumbsucker. And Smith’s music has been celebrated in the world of skateboarding for many years now. Mark Baines skated to “Happiness” in Blueprint’s Waiting for the World in 2000. Nate Broussard used “A Distorted Reality Is Now a Necessity to Be Free” in Static III in 2007. Dylan Rieder skated to “Coast to Coast” in Alien Workshop’s Mind Field in 2009, and Mark Suciu used “Junk Bond Trader” in Cross Continental in 2012.

Arguing that he would have hated the collaboration, aside from being infantilizing, completely ignores the actual culture around Elliott Smith when he was alive, instead opting for some abstract representation of a person who actually existed in the real world — a particularly Gen Z problem, to be frank.

With these collaborations, estates and relevant parties are always compensated. And that actually matters in an era where an artist can remain a staple for young people for generations, long after they’ve died, while reaping diminishing returns thanks to the paltry payouts from streamers. Meaning, short of throwing him onstage as a hologram or doling out money-grabbing box sets, a tasteful collection with a brand with a real connection to his work is actually one of the better options that exists. Trending Stories Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Pink Becomes First Solo Female Pop Artist to Headline the Sphere Judge Sets Steven Tyler Sex Assault Trial Date

People who want to be mad at Supreme want to separate themselves from some sort of mainstream idea of cool. They want to believe that they have an authenticity that Supreme doesn’t. Sorry to say, the reverse is more likely true. Just last week, Business of Fashion published a report on the brand’s cultural durability. “Supreme isn’t cool anymore” is, in fact, a very uncool thing to say in 2026. And beyond that, the people inside the company are coming from an ethos that has remained steadfast in actually supporting youth culture.

To sit on the outside now and pretend that you’re such an authentic Elliott Smith fan that you’re upset by this is simply to expose how out of touch you are.

The growing use of generative AI meant it was "harder to judge what students made themselves."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Australian STEM Videogame Challenge, a "free national videogame development competition for students," has confirmed that the rise of generative AI means this year's event will be the last.

The end of the STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics) Videogame Challenge was first reported on Reddit last week, when someone claiming to be a judge in the program said they'd received an email informing them that "after 12 wonderful years," the 2026 event would mark its end.

Speculation as to the cause of the program's cancellation focused primarily on the presence of AI in entries: Another self-proclaimed judge said the problem wasn't merely the use of AI in itself but that "some students just completely generate their games with AI. How do you mark that? You can't just give them a zero. It's so hard to tell what was or wasn't generated using AI, and especially hard since that's the way the industry is going."

That same person also suggested, however, that funding cutbacks could have been an even bigger factor in the decision, saying, "projects like this are the first to get cut" when government budgets get tight.

Now the Australian Council for Educational Research has confirmed that the cancellation was in fact due to AI.

"After more than a decade inspiring the next generation of young creators, the current 2026 Australian STEM Video Game Challenge will be the competition's final chapter," Cathy Zemaitis, Head of the Student Learning and Progress division at ACER, told PC Gamer.

"The decision was driven by the rapid and complex rise of generative AI in game development, making it harder to judge what students made themselves, and moving the program away from its original purpose."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

So, that's that: A program aimed at encouraging kids to learn has come to an end because the kids learned how to get around the learning part. It's sad, although as a former high school reprobate myself I can't be too critical of the young'uns for doing what they do. I do wonder, though, what all of this might say about our future: Where will we be when we know how to prompt, but not how to create?

Zemaitis added that organizers are not looking at ways to adjusting the program to accommodate generative AI: "There are no plans to pursue alternative options for delivering the Australian STEM Videogame Challenge, however we're always looking for how we can partner with schools and the sector more broadly to deliver programs that enrich the learning experience of students." She also encouraged educators looking for other ways to "keep students challenged" to check out the International Mathematical Modelling Challenge.

Andy has been gaming on PCs from the very beginning, starting as a youngster with text adventures and primitive action games on a cassette-based TRS80. From there he graduated to the glory days of Sierra Online adventures and Microprose sims, ran a local BBS, learned how to build PCs, and developed a longstanding love of RPGs, immersive sims, and shooters. He began writing videogame news in 2007 for The Escapist and somehow managed to avoid getting fired until 2014, when he joined the storied ranks of PC Gamer. He covers all aspects of the industry, from new game announcements and patch notes to legal disputes, Twitch beefs, esports, and Henry Cavill. Lots of Henry Cavill.

‘Sabotage’: Read Hegseth’s Fiery Speech Against His Impeachment

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Politics  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Transcript: ‘Sabotage’: Read Hegseth’s Fiery Speech Against His Impeachment

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth held a rare press conference on Tuesday morning as he faced the prospect of impeachment, delivering an uncompromising and combative speech in defense of his record while excoriating his political opponents.

“What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it,” Hegseth said at the beginning of his remarks.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth held a rare press conference on Tuesday morning as he faced the prospect of impeachment, delivering an uncompromising and combative speech in defense of his record while excoriating his political opponents.

“What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it,” Hegseth said at the beginning of his remarks.

The impeachment effort, led by outgoing Republican Rep. Thomas Massie, largely focuses on the Defense Department chief’s central role in the Iran war but also pertains to other major military actions under his watch—including strikes on alleged drug trafficking boats near Latin America and the operation that led to the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Massie, who introduced eight articles of impeachment against the defense secretary on Monday, accuses Hegseth of waging war against Iran without congressional approval, abusing the powers of his office, and violating federal law and the Constitution.

The House is scheduled to hold an initial procedural vote on impeachment on Tuesday evening.

Below is the full transcript of Hegseth’s speech.—John Haltiwanger

Well, good morning. I’ll get right to it. What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it.

In the 250 years of our American republic, no secretary of war or secretary of defense has ever been impeached for carrying out lawful military operations, and in our case, successful ones at that. Not once, war powers resolution or not. Not under [former U.S. President Ronald] Reagan in Lebanon, not under [former U.S. President Bill] Clinton in Kosovo, not under [former U.S. President Barack] Obama in Iraq and Syria, not under [former U.S. President Joe] Biden in Iraq and Syria. That’s the standard. The history is clear, and it’s very straightforward. And one disgruntled congressman wants to shred it over policy disagreements. That’s what it is: policy disagreements, not high crimes, not misdemeanors, not treason, not bribery, not the kinds of offenses the founders intended when they wrote the impeachment clause.

The proper remedy for a policy disagreement—it’s legislation, it’s debate, it’s the next presidential election. It’s not handing the House and Senate floor to the Democrats so that they can run a show trial against the president and the secretary of war while Republicans still control Congress and should be delivering wins for the American people. You see, we’ve seen this show before. They impeached [U.S.] President [Donald] Trump over a perfect phone call. They impeached him again in his final week in office. Both times they failed. Both times he came back stronger, and the American people sent him back to the White House.

Now, they’re trying a third time. This time against a member of his cabinet. They will fail again, and if standing with President Trump puts me in that company, I’ll take that company every day of the week.

Let’s be clear about what this resolution actually does. It tells our enemies—Iran, the cartels, every adversary watching—that the United States will actually punish its own warriors and its own civilian leadership for successfully executing lawful military operations. It tells them that a single rogue legislator, loaded with personal animus for the president, can attempt to veto national security decisions made by the duly elected commander in chief. It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time. This is not patriotism. This is sabotage.

You know, five years ago, America was humiliated in Kabul, Afghanistan, and every enemy on earth took notice. But somehow, as the president pointed out yesterday—no impeachment vote for [former U.S. Defense Secretary] Lloyd Austin for that terrible withdrawal. Not even one vote. Or no impeachment vote for when he went AWOL for over a week. No impeachment vote for when he let a Chinese spy balloon fly over the country for over a week. See, President Trump ended that kind of wokeness and weakness. Peace through strength, not as a slogan, but as a way of war.

The Houthis found out first. They fired on American ships. Biden had let it happen. President Trump said, “no more.” Operation Rough Rider hit them more than 1,000 times. They cried uncle, and they stopped shooting at our ships to this day. Iran found out next. Operation Midnight Hammer, B-2s from the heartland, halfway around the world, straight into Fordow, Natanz, and Esfahan—flawless. The largest and most successful B-2 strike in American history. Nuclear sites obliterated.

Later in Operation Epic Fury, by every measure, we achieved a historic military victory. Under 40 days of sustained combat, more than 13,000 targets struck. Their navy sunk, their air force grounded, their defense industrial base gone, more than 80 percent of their nuclear industrial base gone, their military in ruin, the ironclad blockade unbroken. We control the Strait of Hormuz. Crazy Islamists in Tehran will never, ever have a nuclear weapon. A historic effort, only made possible by a courageous commander in chief. History will judge the Iran war quite differently than conventional wisdom right now. We didn’t start this war; Iran did, 47 years ago.

But President Trump is willing to do what no other president would. He did the tough, right thing—not for politics, but for the safety and the security of the American people. That’s leadership.

He did not kick the can; he kicked their teeth in. For generations—my generation fought other Middle Eastern wars that dragged on for years, for decades. We lost thousands, thousands of Americans. I wear the bracelet of one of them on my left wrist every single day. It reminds me.

And may I remind you, for perspective, we lost 60,000 Americans in Vietnam over 20 years. We lost 37,000 in Korea in just three years. That many humans are lost on the front lines of Ukraine and Russia every month. This—Epic Fury—has never been that, and it never will be. The media can just never admit the military success because they, you, hate the president more than you love our country or our troops.

Then, in our own hemisphere, we started at home from day one. President Trump made homeland security national security. We sent American troops to lock down the southwest border and choked off the cartel pipeline of human smuggling, of drugs, and death. And then, we took the fight to them. The cartels thought they could just poison the American people and hide from justice. So we formed a counter-cartel coalition, designated them terrorists, and hunted them at sea, and now on land. More than 70 narco-vessels sunk; 218 narcoterrorists dead, where they should be; maritime smuggling down at least 65 percent and dropping even further. Thousands of American lives saved with every drug boat sunk and every shipment prevented.

Indicted and wanted, Nicolás Maduro thought he was untouchable. He was not kidnapped. He was brought to justice by America’s best. Victory in one night. Victory in 48 minutes, to be precise. And today, he sits in front of an American judge in New York City. Flawless success.

Panama kicked China’s Belt and Road Initiative to the curb, and we, along with them, control the canal. The message is simple: This is our hemisphere. Act accordingly.

Our NATO allies, after decades of riding on the American taxpayer in Europe, committed to 5 percent of GDP for their own defense. Our allies are finally stepping up. Out with being a burden, in with burden sharing. Why? Because President Trump demanded it. America is the only country on Earth, and this military the only one, that could successfully execute and lead all of these operations and efforts. Sheer success, victories in both Venezuela and Iran, energy security in our hemisphere, in a way none thought possible for a generation, and no nuclear bomb for Iran ever.

But of course, security doesn’t come free. Nobody was lost in the mission in Venezuela, but 18 Americans have given their lives in Operation Epic Fury. We know their names. I’ve been to Dover. We honor them every day, but their sacrifice only stiffens our resolve.

As it happens, tomorrow is Veterans Day. This year, the operators of Absolute Resolve and the warriors of Epic Fury will mark it for the first time. They stand shoulder to shoulder with the pilots of Midnight Hammer and the sailors of Rough Rider who went before them. They answered the call, they executed, they won, and on the eve of Veterans Day, these resolutions tell every one of them that their service was a crime.

Their service was not a crime. These are heroes, and no politician in Washington will tell them otherwise while I hold this office. President Trump gave this department what it has not had in a generation: a commander in chief with the courage to lead and the resolve to win. I stand behind every one of these operations, I was proud to execute them, and I would do it again tomorrow.

And while this defeated politician—defeated ironically by a Navy SEAL, I might add—while this defeated politician drafts articles of impeachment, we’ve delivered the first increase, and a big one, in hostile fire and imminent danger pay in more than 20 years. We’ve reinstated over 600 service members who were forced out under the previous administration’s COVID[-19] mandate. All five of our services broke historic recruiting records: the Army, the Navy, the Air Force, the Space Force, the Marines for two years in a row. In fact, the Marines in FY27 met half of their goal on day one. Americans are lining up to serve in President Trump’s Department of War. And by the way, to those Marines and all those Marines out there, semper fi and happy 251st birthday today.

Two years of historic recruitment after Biden couldn’t recruit anyone. Same with retention, by the way, through the roof. That means the troops that are in are staying in. The best of the best, based on merit, staying in. Night and day from Biden to Trump. We cut unnecessary senior officer billets by 20 percent and raised combat performance standards. Through the One Big Beautiful Bill, we’re investing in troop pay, in housing, in healthcare, in advanced weapons and equipment our warfighters actually need to win. That’s the record. That’s what we’re defending.

Make no mistake about what is happening. This is not about me. Politicians in Washington have accused the sailors and the pilots who struck narcoterrorist boats of war crimes for doing their job. Senators in cute videos have suggested the same. This resolution is the next step. Put the secretary on trial today, and the men and women who carried out those orders tomorrow. If they want to come for the warriors, they’re going to have to come through me.

As for the articles themselves, Iran, the cartels, Maduro, Yemen. Every one was a lawful operation ordered by the commander in chief, then ordered by myself, notified to Congress, and executed by American warriors who did their jobs. We destroyed terror regimes’ capabilities. We sank the boats carrying poison into our communities. We put a narco-dictator in handcuffs. These are not high crimes. This is the job, and it’s the job well done.

For the first time in American history, a cabinet secretary overseeing America’s military is being attacked not for losing conflicts with our enemies, but for winning them. Condemned not for defeat, but for military victory. The disgruntled congressman even admitted this himself yesterday. No failure here, just impeachment for success.

As to the claim that the American military targets civilians, well, that is a lie and that is a slander against every professional who flew, sailed, and fought on these missions. The only people who target civilians are the terrorists that we hunt, and I will dignify this claim no further. None of these are high crimes or misdemeanors; they’re partisan policy disagreements from people who want to politicize our military.

We will not apologize for putting American lives first. We will not apologize for stopping the flow of drugs that kill our citizens. We will not apologize for destroying the capabilities of a regime that chants “Death to America,” and if that regime makes one wrong move, as the president said, we remain locked and loaded with the full weight and lethality of Central Command. We will not apologize for restoring a military that recruits based on merit, restores real standards, and fights to win.

These resolutions are a farce. They mock the Constitution. They attack the chain of command, and they criminalize the warfighters who execute lawful orders. This process hands our adversaries a propaganda victory and wastes the time of Congress that should instead be focused on protecting the American people.

We will continue to do the job the American people elected President Trump to do: protect this country, defeat our enemies, and rebuild a military worthy of the warriors who wear the uniform.

I pray each day, and I hope you do too, for our troops. They are the best of our nation. Like the president, I will defend them with every fiber of my being, every single day. That includes having their back, no matter what. So to the troops, as I’ve said before: Don’t blink, ignore the noise, never hesitate. This is all politics. You do real things. Stay the course. May Almighty God protect them always, and may our actions today and every day bring glory to Him. We will stand, we will fight, and we will defend this republic against all enemies, foreign and domestic. And amen.

Airport Lounges Are Packed—Travelers Demand $5,000 Annual Credit Card Fees To Keep The Masses Out

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Airport lounges are so crowded that travelers are demanding $200 admission prices and $5,000 annual credit card fees to keep the “untouchables” out, arguing that the “true lounge caste” has been driven into the concourse while everyone else crowds around the buffet. There are also lounges where the line outside and the crowd inside make the benefit nearly worthless. Paying for somewhere comfortable to sit, and then being unable to find a seat, has become a really legitimate complaint – even if it’s mixed with real snobbery because it’s the ‘other’ people who shouldn’t be in there. “Premium” travel experience before 10am on a Sunday https://t.co/13PniAuksG — David Slotnick (@David_Slotnick) November 8, 2026 Status anxiety places airline lounges belong alongside BMWs, remote work, and ski trips as things that have lost their cachet as access spread. They’re still aspirational to the people getting in, while the people already inside are unhappy that increased access has lowered their relative status. View wyatt.today’s post on Twitter The original poster’s follow-up invokes the tens of thousands of his employer’s dollars he spends on flights, then uses a racial slur for other guests. It’s no longer about finding empty space inside. But most people just want a chance to use the benefit they’re paying for and maybe a snack without standing in line in the terminal like a schmuck to get an $895 ‘free’ drink. Wanting Quiet Space Isn’t Elitist One reply argues that a lounge defeats its purpose when it gets too crowded. It may still have food, a shower, or an airline agent to help when your flight gets delayed or cancels. But the core value proposition is escape from the terminal. I often just want a place to open my laptop without balancing it on my knees. And then having frictionless access to a snack, and the ability to run into the washroom without bringing all my stuff with me, are cherries on top. Nobel laureate James Buchanan’s theory of clubs explains this well. Sharing a facility spreads its costs across more people. Eventually, adding people creates enough congestion to make the experience worse. The membership and the size of the facility have to be considered together. There need to be access limits to preserve the value proposition of the facility. But since most people don’t use the space most of the time, it’s perceived option value that they’re purchasing and it continues to drive premium credit card acquisition and adoption. Lounges Got Better, And People Started Using Them Differently I’ve written about the claim that airport lounge culture represents American decline. The nostaglic view is just wrong. I remember ginger ale and Goldfish crackers, a photocopier in the Admirals Club, and packaged Tillamook cheese in United’s Red Carpet Clubs. Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine. The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up. People go for the food People who might have stopped in to check e-mail now plan to eat a meal. They come more often and stay longer. A better lounge becomes a destination in its own right. The travel mix has changed. Traditional managed business travel is a smaller part of the story. Premium leisure travelers, families, and people combining work with a trip use the same spaces. A Monday – Thursday consultant arriving just before boarding and a family building in a generous airport buffer spend very different amounts of time. Credit cards have expanded access. That brings in more people, including travelers who wouldn’t buy a separate airline club membership. Uncertain security waits get people to the airport earlier. If security might take ten minutes or an hour, you allow for the hour. When it takes ten minutes, you’ve suddenly got time to spend. Even without an increase in the average security wait, greater uncertainty can mean more time in the airport. I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections. A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience. You Could Buy Your Way Into Lounges In The 1960s, Too One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. Topics on this pageAirport loungeCredit cardAmerican AirlinesAmerican ExpressBMWCapital OneChaseChase BankDavid SlotnickDelta Air LinesDenverHeathrow AirportLaGuardia AirportLas VegasPriority PassTillamookUnited Airlines+15 more More From View from the Wing

Paying for somewhere comfortable to sit, and then being unable to find a seat, has become a really legitimate complaint – even if it’s mixed with real snobbery because it’s the ‘other’ people who shouldn’t be in there.

A better lounge becomes a destination in its own right. The travel mix has changed. Traditional managed business travel is a smaller part of the story. Premium leisure travelers, families, and people combining work with a trip use the same spaces. If security might take ten minutes or an hour, you allow for the hour. When it takes ten minutes, you’ve suddenly got time to spend. A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. Topics on this pageAirport loungeCredit cardAmerican AirlinesAmerican ExpressBMWCapital OneChaseChase BankDavid SlotnickDelta Air LinesDenverHeathrow AirportLaGuardia AirportLas VegasPriority PassTillamookUnited Airlines+15 more More From View from the Wing

I’ve written about the claim that airport lounge culture represents American decline. The nostaglic view is just wrong. I remember ginger ale and Goldfish crackers, a photocopier in the Admirals Club, and packaged Tillamook cheese in United’s Red Carpet Clubs.

Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine.

The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up.

I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections.

A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge.

I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience.

I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience.

One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Bandai Namco is liquidating Unknown 9: Awakening studio Reflector Entertainment, following multiple previous layoffs since the game's launch

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As another addition to the slew of smaller studios being dissolved by their parent companies, Bandai Namco announced in a press release that they are shutting down their subsidiary studio Reflector Entertainment, a Montreal studio responsible for 2024's action-adventure game Unknown 9: Awakening. In its notice, Bandai Namco explains that the reason for the liquidation is due to Reflector not meeting its expectations for "future business plans, necessary additional investment, and the possibility of profitability and business continuation."

The press release notes that the liquidation of Reflector will result in an estimated "extraordinary loss" of around 21 million euros, or 3.5 billion yen, for Bandai Namco in its Q3 fiscal report of this year. This is not the first time Reflector has been cut by Bandai, as around a month after the launch of Unknown 9: Awakening and presumably in response to its low sales, 18% of the workforce was laid off. 2025 then saw further redundancies amidst the cancellation of a prospective project, though details on those layoffs were not granted by the company. Unknown 9 was intended to be a transmedia franchise involving the game as well as comic books and novels, but these ambitions never came to pass.

Bandai Namco notes that the liquidation will be held to local laws and procedures, which make the actual schedule of the process uncertain for now. A similar process faces Arkane Lyon, which is currently in a purgatory of having been chosen for closure by Microsoft, but temporarily protected by French labor laws that prevent it from being suddenly closed without proper negotiation and procedure.

Mamdani gives nonprofits first dibs on buying apartment buildings

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mayor Zohran Mamdani signed COPA, requiring owners to notify the city and qualified nonprofits before selling covered buildings. The law takes effect in 1 year, as a 38 building portfolio with 850 mostly rent stabilized units heads to foreclosure auctions.  AI Summary

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani signed legislation Tuesday giving vetted nonprofits a first shot at buying troubled city apartment buildings.

The new measure arrived too late to spare hundreds of rent-stabilized units from foreclosure, a second loss in Mamdani’s bid to protect such units.

Mamdani ran for office and won on creating and protecting affordable housing. National attention has since focused on his efforts to build more housing and cut red tape. His political inspiration and influence have spread to other parts of the country.

Nithya Raman is a prime example. Under a similar affordability theme, the Los Angeles City Council member, also a member of the Democratic Socialists of America, ousted an incumbent to become L.A. mayor.

The Community Opportunity to Purchase Act, known as COPA, requires owners of covered buildings to notify the city and qualified nonprofits before selling. Those groups then get time to make an offer and a right to match a later one. The law takes effect one year after enactment, after the Department of Housing Preservation and Development writes rules.

“This law will give New Yorkers a tool to keep their homes out of the hands of predatory investors seeking to make a quick and easy buck,” Mamdani said at a signing event Tuesday.

He also won in court last week. A Brooklyn appellate panel ruled Nov. 4 that the administration can resume collecting its pied-à-terre tax on high-end second homes, reversing a Staten Island judge who found the rollout flawed. Mamdani says the tax could raise $500 million a year. Other legal challenges remain.

But his administration could not stop the sale of a 38-building portfolio with 850 mostly rent-stabilized apartments. Spatial Equity, an affordable housing developer with City Hall’s backing, failed to close a deal to buy the portfolio before an East Harlem auction began Tuesday afternoon.

The 38 buildings belonged to bankrupt Emerald Equities Group. Banks and other institutions that control about $250 million in mortgage loans will foreclose on each building over three auction days.

Lenders rejected Spatial Equity’s initial $68 million bid, according to a City Limits report. They also turned down a $110 million offer last month, tenant leader Cara Polte told Gothamist.

Polte said tenants will keep pushing for a buyer committed to fixing the buildings. Tenants had hoped to turn her building and four others on the block into a community land trust.

A trust managed by several banks and by Sabal Investment Holdings controls the loans. Freddie Mac partially insured them. Sabal has yet to respond to media requests.

Cea Weaver, director of the Mayor’s Office to Protect Tenants, said the sale does not end the city’s effort to force owners to improve conditions.

This setback follows Mamdani’s first loss on this front. On Jan. 1, he stood in a Pinnacle-owned building and vowed to take on bad landlords. He then sought a 30-day delay of the bankruptcy auction of Pinnacle’s rent-regulated portfolio, hoping to find a buyer other than Summit Properties USA.

A judge allowed the sale to proceed. Summit won about 5,100 mostly rent-stabilized units from Pinnacle, which had filed for Chapter 11 after Flagstar Bank foreclosed.

Emerald bought the 38 buildings for $350 million in 2016 and began raising rents on stabilized apartments. It took out another $180 million loan in December 2018, six months before state lawmakers passed tenant protections that outlawed many of those tactics.

Lenders foreclosed in 2024. Emerald filed for bankruptcy a year later.

A state investigation found Emerald had illegally removed 21 apartments from rent stabilization. The buildings had more than 2,300 housing code violations in April. A retaining wall behind buildings on East 103rd Street collapsed earlier this year, days before Mamdani and Weaver visited in March.

About 1,600 rent-stabilized buildings face financial distress, according to Rent Guidelines Board data. Landlord groups blame rent restrictions, including the board’s two-year rent freeze. Tenant advocates say owners like Emerald borrowed heavily expecting rent increases that later law barred.

In June, Mamdani won a rent freeze through the Rent Guidelines Board for the city’s roughly 1 million stabilized apartments.

Landlords sued to reverse the freeze. The case began in Staten Island, but Justice Ralph Porzio moved it to Manhattan in August, handing Mamdani a partial win. In September, the judge ordered City Hall to turn over communications with the board and signaled he would likely not rule by Oct. 1.

Oct. 1 came and went without a ruling, and the freeze took effect for new and renewing leases.

Meanwhile, a federal lawsuit remains pending over a 2019 state law that limits rents on stabilized units. Small property owners sued in November 2025, arguing the law unconstitutionally keeps stabilized apartments vacant.

COPA’s path was uneven until Mamdani took office. The City Council passed an earlier version in December 2025, but then-Mayor Eric Adams vetoed it, and the Council did not override him. A revised, narrower version passed Oct. 22.

“Progress … is something that we celebrate, and also, we know that it is not inevitable,” Mamdani said. “It has to be won.”
